**Figure 12: Combination of Control Rules and Reference Points for Bottomfish and Seamount Groundfish Stocks** 

#### **5.4 Management Program for Precious Corals Fisheries**

No precious corals harvester has received a federal permit to harvest corals from the EEZ surrounding American Samoa since the implementation of the Precious Corals FMP in 1980, however, this does not preclude any future permit issuance.

--- SENTENCE 1606 ---
The U.S. EEZ surrounding American Samoa has been defined, for the purposes of precious coral fisheries management, as an Exploratory Precious Coral Permit Area.

#### **5.4.1 Permits**

Any vessel of the United States fishing for, taking or retaining precious corals in any precious corals permit area must have a permit.

--- SENTENCE 1607 ---
Each permit will be valid for fishing only in the permit area.

--- SENTENCE 1608 ---
No more than one permit will be valid for any one person at any one time.

--- SENTENCE 1609 ---
The holder of a valid permit to fish one permit area may obtain a permit to fish another permit area only upon surrendering to the NMFS Regional Administrator any current permit for the precious corals fishery.

#### **5.4.2 Seasons and Quotas**

The fishing year for precious corals begins on July 1 and ends on June 30 the following year.

--- SENTENCE 1610 ---
The quota limiting the amount of precious corals that may be taken in any precious corals permit area in an exploratory area during the fishing year is 1,000 kg per area, all species combined (except black corals).

--- SENTENCE 1611 ---
Only live coral is counted toward the quota.

--- SENTENCE 1612 ---
Live coral means any precious coral that has live coral polyps or tissue.

--- SENTENCE 1613 ---
The quotas for exploratory areas will be held in reserve for harvest by vessels of the U.S. by determining at the beginning of each fishing year that the reserve for each of the three exploratory areas will equal the quota minus the estimated domestic annual harvest for that year.

--- SENTENCE 1614 ---
And, as soon as practicable after December 31, each year, the Regional Administrator will determine the amount harvested by vessels of the U.S. between July 1 and December 31 of that year.

--- SENTENCE 1615 ---
NMFS will release to TALFF an amount of precious coral for each exploratory area equal to the quota minus the two times amount harvested by vessels of the U.S. in that July 1 to December 31 period.

--- SENTENCE 1616 ---
Finally, NMFS will publish in the Federal Register a notification of the Regional Administrator's determination and a summary of the information of which it is based a soon as practicable after the determination is made.

## **5.4.3 Closures**

If the NMFS Regional Administrator determines that the harvest quota for any coral bed will be reached prior to the end of the fishing year, NMFS will issue a Federal Register notice closing the bed and the public will be informed through appropriate news media.

--- SENTENCE 1617 ---
Any such field order must indicate the reason for the closure, delineate the bed being closed, and identify the effective date of the closure.

--- SENTENCE 1618 ---
A closure is also effective for a permit holder upon the permit holder's actual harvest of the applicable quota.

#### **5.4.4 Restrictions**

*Size Restrictions*--The height of a live coral specimen shall be determined by a straight line measurement taken from its base to its most distal extremity.

--- SENTENCE 1619 ---
The stem diameter of a living coral specimen shall be determined by measuring the greatest diameter of the stem at a point no less than one inch (2.54 cm) from the top surface of the living holdfast.

--- SENTENCE 1620 ---
Live pink coral harvested from any precious corals permit area must have attained a minimum height of 10 inches (25.4 cm).

--- SENTENCE 1621 ---
Live black coral harvested from any precious corals permit area must have attained either a minimum stem diameter of 1 inch (2.54 cm), or a minimum height of 48 inches (122 cm).

--- SENTENCE 1622 ---
*Gear Restrictions*-- Only selective gear may be used to harvest coral from any precious corals permit area.

--- SENTENCE 1623 ---
Selective gear means any gear used for harvesting corals that can discriminate or differentiate between type, size, quality, or characteristics of living or dead corals.

#### **5.4.5 Framework Procedures**

Established management measures may be revised and new management measures may be established and/or revised through rulemaking if new information demonstrates that there are biological, social, or economic concerns in a precious corals permit area.

--- SENTENCE 1624 ---
By June 30 of each year, the Council-appointed Precious Corals Plan Team will prepare an annual report on the fishery in the management area.

--- SENTENCE 1625 ---
The report will contain, among other things, recommendations for Council action and an assessment of the urgency and effects of such action(s).

--- SENTENCE 1626 ---
Established measures are management measures that, at some time, have been included in regulations implementing the FMP, and for which the impacts have been evaluated in Council/NMFS documents in the context of current conditions.

--- SENTENCE 1627 ---
According to the framework procedures of Amendment 3 to the FMP, the Council may recommend to the Regional Administrator that established measures be modified, removed, or re-instituted.

--- SENTENCE 1628 ---
Such recommendation will include supporting rationale and analysis and will be made after advance public notice, public discussion, and consideration of public comment.

--- SENTENCE 1629 ---
NMFS may implement the Council's recommendation by rulemaking if approved by the Regional Administrator.

--- SENTENCE 1630 ---
New measures are management measures that have not been included in regulations implementing the FMP, or for which the impacts have not been evaluated in Council/NMFS documents in the context of current conditions.

--- SENTENCE 1631 ---
Following the framework procedures of Amendment 3 to the FMP, the Council will publicize, including by a Federal Register document, and solicit public comment on, any proposed new management measure.

--- SENTENCE 1632 ---
After a Council meeting at which the measure is discussed, the Council will consider recommendations and prepare a Federal Register document summarizing the Council's deliberations, rationale, and analysis for the preferred action and the time and place for any subsequent Council meeting(s) to consider the new measure.

--- SENTENCE 1633 ---
At a subsequent public meeting, the Council will consider public comments and other information received before making a recommendation to the Regional Administrator about any new measure.

--- SENTENCE 1634 ---
If approved by the Regional Administrator, NMFS may implement the Council's recommendation by rulemaking.

#### **5.4.6 Bycatch Measures**

A variety of invertebrates and fish are known to utilize the same habitat as precious corals.

--- SENTENCE 1635 ---
Such organisms include *onaga* (*Etelis coruscans*), *kāhala* (*Seriola dumerallii*) and the shrimp *Heterocarpus ensifer*, however, there is no evidence that these species or others significantly depend on precious coral beds for shelter or food.

--- SENTENCE 1636 ---
In addition, only selective gear can be used to harvest precious corals, thereby reducing the potential for bycatch.

#### **5.4.7 Application of National Standard 1**

Due to the paucity of information on the existence and distribution of precious corals and the absence of a precious coral fishery in the American Samoa Archipelago, specification of MSY, OY and overfishing have not been specifically determined for precious coral management unit species.

--- SENTENCE 1637 ---
However, as a precautionary approach, a quota for precious corals in the Exploratory Precious Coral Permit Area has been set at 1,000 kg/year.

--- SENTENCE 1638 ---
Should a precious coral fishery develop in the American Samoa Archipelago, the Council may develop specifications for specific coral beds depending on the information and stock assessment tools available.

#### **Measures to address overfishing**

At present no stocks of precious corals have been determined to be overfished or that overfishing is occurring.

--- SENTENCE 1639 ---
Provisions of the Precious Corals FMP, as amended, are sufficient to prevent overfishing and these measures will be carried over into the FEP.

#### **5.5 Management Program for Crustacean Fisheries**

#### **5.5.1 Management Areas and Subareas**

Permit Area 3 is the EEZ around the Territory of American Samoa and the EEZ around the Territory of Guam.

--- SENTENCE 1640 ---
Permit Areas 1 and 2 are waters of the EEZ around the Hawaiian Islands.

#### **5.5.2 Permits and Reporting Requirements**

Federal permit and logbook reporting is required when fishing for lobsters in the US EEZ around American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1641 ---
A permit must obtain from the Regional Administrator and will be issued to the owner of the vessel that is used to fish for lobster.

#### **5.5.3 Gear Restrictions**

In Permit Area 3, it is unlawful for any person to fish for, take or retain lobsters with explosives, poisons, or electrical shocking devices.

#### **5.5.4 Notifications**

Vessel operators must report not less than 24 hours, but not more than 36 hours, before landing, the port, the approximate date and the approximate time at which spiny and slipper lobsters will be landed.

--- SENTENCE 1642 ---
They must also report not less than six hours, and not more than twelve hours, before offloading, the location and time that offloading spiny and slipper lobsters will begin.

--- SENTENCE 1643 ---
The Regional Administrator will notify permit holders of any change in the reporting method and schedule required at least 30 days prior to the opening of the fishing season.

#### **5.5.5 At-sea Observer Coverage**

All fishing vessels must carry an observer when requested to do so by the NMFS Regional Administrator.

#### **5.5.6 Framework Procedures**

New management measures may be added through rulemaking if new information demonstrates that there are biological, social, or economic concerns in Permit Areas 1, 2 or 3.

--- SENTENCE 1644 ---
By June 30 of each year, the Council-appointed Plan Team will prepare an annual report on the fisheries in the management area.

--- SENTENCE 1645 ---
The report shall contain, among other things, recommendations for Council action and an assessment of the urgency and effects of such action(s).

--- SENTENCE 1646 ---
Established measures are management measures that, at some time, have been included in regulations implementing the FMP, and for which the impacts have been evaluated in Council/NMFS documents in the context of current conditions.

--- SENTENCE 1647 ---
Following the framework procedures of Amendment 9 to the FMP, the Council may recommend to the NMFS Regional Administrator that established measures be modified, removed, or re-instituted.

--- SENTENCE 1648 ---
Such recommendation shall include supporting rationale and analysis, and shall be made after advance public notice, public discussion, and consideration of public comment.

--- SENTENCE 1649 ---
NMFS may implement the Council's recommendation by rulemaking if approved by the Regional Administrator.

--- SENTENCE 1650 ---
New measures are management measures that have not been included in regulations implementing the FMP, or for which the impacts have not been evaluated in Council/NMFS documents in the context of current conditions.

--- SENTENCE 1651 ---
Following the framework procedures of Amendment 9 to the FMP, the Council will publicize, including by a Federal Register document, and solicit public comment on, any proposed new management measure.

--- SENTENCE 1652 ---
After a Council meeting at which the measure is discussed, the Council will consider recommendations and prepare a Federal Register document summarizing the Council's deliberations, rationale, and analysis for the preferred action, and the time and place for any subsequent Council meeting(s) to consider the new measure.

--- SENTENCE 1653 ---
At subsequent public meeting(s), the Council will consider public comments and other information received to make a recommendation to the Regional Administrator about any new measure.

--- SENTENCE 1654 ---
NMFS may implement the Council's recommendation by rulemaking if approved by the Regional Administrator.

#### **5.5.7 Bycatch Measures**

No bycatch measures or actions are necessary at this time.

--- SENTENCE 1655 ---
Lobsters are taken by hand and harvest occurs almost exclusively in territorial waters, 0-3 miles.

--- SENTENCE 1656 ---
There is no bycatch associated with this fishery.

#### **5.5.8 Application of National Standard 1**

Specification of MSY, OY and overfishing have not been determined for crustacean management unit species in American Samoa as there is virtually no crustaceans fishery operating in the EEZ surrounding those areas at present.

--- SENTENCE 1657 ---
However, should a crustacean fishery develop, and the Council determine a stock status determination is needed, the Council will rely on the specification of MSY, OY and overfishing, including target and rebuilding control rules and reference points established for the NWHI lobster fishery until appropriate specifications are developed for crustacean fishery resources of the American Samoa Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1658 ---
The specifications would be applied to multi-species stock complexes or to individual species, depending on the information and stock assessment tools available.

#### **5.6 Management Program for Coral Reef Ecosystem Fisheries**

#### **5.6.1 Marine Protected Areas (MPAs)**

Rose Atoll in American Samoa is a designated MPA with an outer boundary at the 50-fm isobath.

--- SENTENCE 1659 ---
It is a no-take MPA in which all extractive activities are prohibited except for small harvests related to scientific research and related resource management.

#### **5.6.2 Permits and Reporting Requirements**

Any person who harvests coral reef ecosystem MUS in a low-use MPAs is required to have a Federal special permit issued by NMFS.

--- SENTENCE 1660 ---
Issuance of special permits is on a case-by-case basis and based upon several factors including the potential for bycatch, the sensitivity of the area to the type of fishing proposed, and the level of fishing occurring in relation to the level considered sustainable in a low-use MPA.

--- SENTENCE 1661 ---
A person permitted and targeting non-CRE MUS under other fishery management plans is not required to obtain a special permit to fish in low-use MPAs.

--- SENTENCE 1662 ---
In addition to the permit requirement for low-use MPAs, special permits are required for any directed fisheries on potentially harvested coral reef taxa (PHCRT) within the regulatory area or to fish for any CRE MUS in the coral reef regulatory area with any gear not normally permitted.

--- SENTENCE 1663 ---
Those issued a permit to fish within one of the other FMPs who incidentally catches CRE MUS while fishing for the other MUS is exempt from the permit requirement of this FEP.

--- SENTENCE 1664 ---
Also exempt from the permit requirement are those fishing for currently harvested coral reef taxa (CHCRT) outside of a MPA who does not retain any incidentally-caught PHCRT, and any person collecting marine organisms for scientific research.

--- SENTENCE 1665 ---
Permits are only valid for fishing in the fishery management subarea specified on the permit.

--- SENTENCE 1666 ---
The harvest of live rock and living corals is prohibited throughout the federally managed U.S. EEZ waters of the region; however, under special permits with conditions specified by NMFS following consultation with the Council, indigenous people could be allowed to harvest live rock or coral for traditional uses, and aquaculture operations could be permitted to harvest seed stock.

--- SENTENCE 1667 ---
A Federal reporting system for all fishing under special permits is in place.

--- SENTENCE 1668 ---
Resource monitoring systems administered by state, territorial, and commonwealth agencies continue to collect fishery data on the existing coral reef fisheries that do not require special permits.

#### **5.6.3 Notification**

Any special permit holder must contact the appropriate NMFS enforcement agent in American Samoa at least 24 hours before landing any CRE MUS harvested under a special permit, and report the port and the approximate date and time at which the catch will be landed.

#### **5.6.4 Gear Restrictions**

Allowable gear types include: (1) Hand harvest; (2) spear; (3) slurp gun; (4) hand/dip net; (5) hoop net for Kona crab; (6) throw net; (7) barrier net; (8) surround/purse net that is attended at all times; (9) hook-and-line (powered and unpowered handlines, rod and reel, and trolling); (10) crab and fish traps with vessel ID number affixed; and (11) remote operating vehicles/submersibles.

--- SENTENCE 1669 ---
New fishing gears that are not included in the allowable gear list may be allowed under the special permit provision.

--- SENTENCE 1670 ---
CRE MUS may not be taken by means of poisons, explosives, or intoxicating substances.

--- SENTENCE 1671 ---
Possession and use of these materials is prohibited.

--- SENTENCE 1672 ---
All fish and crab trap gear used by permit holders must be identified with the vessel number.

--- SENTENCE 1673 ---
Unmarked traps and unattended surround nets or bait seine nets found deployed will be considered unclaimed property and may be disposed of by NMFS or other authorized officers.

#### **5.6.5 Framework Procedures**

A framework process, providing for an administratively simplified procedure to facilitate adjustments to management measures previously analyzed in the CRE FMP, is an important component of the FEP.

--- SENTENCE 1674 ---
These framework measures include designating "no-anchoring" zones and establishing mooring buoys, requiring vessel monitoring systems on board fishing vessels, designating areas for the sole use of indigenous peoples, and moving species from the PHCRT to the CHCRT list when sufficient data has been collected.

--- SENTENCE 1675 ---
A general fishing permit program could also be established for all U.S. EEZ coral reef ecosystem fisheries under the framework process.

#### **5.6.6 Other Actions**

There are other non-regulatory measures consistent with plan objectives that are being undertaken by the Council outside of the regulatory regime.

--- SENTENCE 1676 ---
These include a process and criteria for EFH consultations; formal plan team coordination to identify and to address coral reef ecosystem impacts from existing fisheries; a system to facilitate consistent state and territorial level management; and research and education efforts.

#### **5.6.7 Bycatch Measures**

Almost all coral reef fishes caught in American Samoa are considered food fishes and are kept, regardless or size or species.

--- SENTENCE 1677 ---
There is no specific information available on bycatch from coral reef fisheries, particularly inshore fisheries.

#### **5.6.8 Application of National Standard 1**

#### **MSY Control Rule and Stock Status Determination**

Available biological and fishery data are poor for all coral reef ecosystem management unit species in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1678 ---
There is scant information on the life histories, ecosystem dynamics, fishery impact, community structure changes, yield potential, and management reference points for many coral reef ecosystem species.

--- SENTENCE 1679 ---
Additionally, total fishing effort cannot be adequately partitioned between the various management unit species (MUS) for any fishery or area.

--- SENTENCE 1680 ---
Biomass, maximum sustainable yield, and fishing mortality estimates are not available for any single MUS.

--- SENTENCE 1681 ---
Once these data are available, fishery managers will then be able to establish limits and reference points based on the multi-species coral reef ecosystem as a whole.

--- SENTENCE 1682 ---
When possible, the MSY control rule should be applied to the individual species in a multispecies stock.

--- SENTENCE 1683 ---
When this is not possible, MSY may be specified for one or more species; these values can then be used as indicators for the multi-species stock's MSY.

--- SENTENCE 1684 ---
Clearly, any given species that is part of a multi-species complex will respond differently to an OY-determined level of fishing effort (FOY).

--- SENTENCE 1685 ---
Thus, for a species complex that is fished at FOY, managers still must track individual species' mortality rates in order to prevent species-specific population declines that would lead to strict protection, as required by the Endangered Species Act.

--- SENTENCE 1686 ---
For the coral reef fisheries, the multi-species complex as a whole is used to establish limits and reference points for each area.

--- SENTENCE 1687 ---
When possible, available data for a particular species will be used to evaluate the status of individual MUS stocks in order to prevent recruitment overfishing.

--- SENTENCE 1688 ---
When better data and the appropriate multi-species stock assessment methodologies become available, all stocks will be evaluated independently, without proxy.

#### **Establishing Reference Point Values**

Standardized values of catch per unit effort (CPUE) and effort (E) are used to establish limit and reference point values, which act as proxies for relative biomass and fishing mortality, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 1689 ---
Limits and reference points are calculated in terms of CPUEMSY and EMSY included in Table 10.

--- SENTENCE 1690 ---
**Table 10: CPUE-based Overfishing Limits and Reference Points for Coral Reef Species** 

| Value

--- SENTENCE 1691 ---
| Proxy        | Explanation                             |
|---------------|--------------|-----------------------------------------|
| MaxFMT (FMSY)

--- SENTENCE 1692 ---
| EMSY         | 0.91

--- SENTENCE 1693 ---
CPUEMSY                            |
| FOY           | 0.75 EMSY    | suggested default scaling for target    |
| BMSY          | CPUEMSY      | operational counterpart                 |
| BOY           | 1.3 CPUEMSY  | simulation results from Mace (1994)

--- SENTENCE 1694 ---
| 0.7 CPUEMSY  | suggested default (1-M)BMSY with M=0.3* |
| BFLAG         | 0.91 CPUEMSY | suggested default (1-M)BOY with M=0.3*  |

--- SENTENCE 1695 ---
When reliable estimates of EMSY and CPUEMSY are not available, they are estimated from the available time series of catch and effort values, standardized for all identifiable biases using the best available analytical tools.

--- SENTENCE 1696 ---
CPUEMSY is calculated as one-half a multi-year moving average reference CPUE (CPUEREF).

#### **Measures to Address Overfishing and Overfished Stocks**

At present, no CRE stocks in the American Samoa Archipelago have been determined to be overfished or that overfishing is occurring.

--- SENTENCE 1697 ---
If in the future it is determined that overfishing is occurring, a stock is, or either of those two conditions is being approached, the Council will establish additional management measures.

--- SENTENCE 1698 ---
Measures that may be considered include additional area closures, seasonal closures, establishment of limited access systems, limits on catch per trip, limits on effort per trip, and fleet-wide limits on catch or effort.

--- SENTENCE 1699 ---
While managing the multi-species stocks to provide maximum benefit, fishery managers must also ensure that the resulting fishing mortality rate does not reduce any individual species stock to a level requiring protection under the Endangered Species Act.

--- SENTENCE 1700 ---
Preventing recruitment overfishing on any component stock will satisfy this need in a precautionary manner.

--- SENTENCE 1701 ---
Best available data are used for each fishery to estimate these values.

--- SENTENCE 1702 ---
These reference points will be related primarily to recruitment overfishing and will be expressed in units such as spawning potential ratio or spawning stock biomass.

--- SENTENCE 1703 ---
However, no examples can be provided at present.

--- SENTENCE 1704 ---
Species for which managers have collected extensive survey data and know their life history parameters, such as growth rate and size at reproduction, are the best candidates for determining these values.

--- SENTENCE 1705 ---
Using the best available data, managers will monitor changes in species abundance and/or composition.

--- SENTENCE 1706 ---
They will pay special attention to those species they consider important because of their trophic level or other ecological importance to the larger community.

# **CHAPTER 6: IDENTIFICATION AND DESCRIPTION OF ESSENTIAL FISH HABITAT**

#### **6.1 Introduction**

In 1996, Congress passed the Sustainable Fisheries Act, which amended the MSA and added several new FMP provisions.

--- SENTENCE 1707 ---
From an ecosystem management perspective, the identification and description of EFH for all federally managed species were among the most important of these additions.

--- SENTENCE 1708 ---
According to the MSA, EFH is defined as "those waters and substrate necessary to fish for spawning, breeding or growth to maturity."

--- SENTENCE 1709 ---
This new mandate represented a significant shift in fishery management.

--- SENTENCE 1710 ---
Because the provision required councils to consider a MUS's ecological role and habitat requirements in managing fisheries, it allowed Councils to move beyond the traditional single-species or multispecies management to a broader ecosystem-based approach.

--- SENTENCE 1711 ---
In 1999, NMFS issued guidelines intended to assist Councils in implementing the EFH provision of the MSA, and set forth the following four broad tasks:

- 1.

--- SENTENCE 1712 ---
Identify and describe EFH for all species managed under an FMP.
- 2.

--- SENTENCE 1713 ---
Describe adverse impacts to EFH from fishing activities.
- 3.

--- SENTENCE 1714 ---
Describe adverse impacts to EFH from non-fishing activities.
- 4.

--- SENTENCE 1715 ---
Recommend conservation and enhancement measures to minimize and mitigate the adverse impacts to EFH resulting from fishing and non–fishing related activities.

--- SENTENCE 1716 ---
The guidelines recommended that each Council prepare a preliminary inventory of available environmental and fisheries information on each managed species.

--- SENTENCE 1717 ---
Such an inventory is useful in describing and identifying EFH, as it also helps to identify missing information about the habitat utilization patterns of particular species.

--- SENTENCE 1718 ---
The guidelines note that a wide range of basic information is needed to identify EFH.

--- SENTENCE 1719 ---
This includes data on current and historic stock size, the geographic range of the managed species, the habitat requirements by life history stage, and the distribution and characteristics of those habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1720 ---
Because EFH has to be identified for each major life history stage, information about a species' distribution, density, growth, mortality, and production within all of the habitats it occupies, or formerly occupied, is also necessary.

--- SENTENCE 1721 ---
The guidelines also state that the quality of available data used to identify EFH should be rated using the following four-level system:

| Level 1: | All that is known is where a species occurs based on distribution data for |
|----------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|          | all or part of the geographic range of the species.

--- SENTENCE 1722 ---
|

- Level 2: Data on habitat-related densities or relative abundance of the species are available.

--- SENTENCE 1723 ---
- Level 3: Data on growth, reproduction, or survival rates within habitats are available.

#### Level 4: Production rates by habitat are available.

--- SENTENCE 1724 ---
With higher quality data, those habitats most highly valued by a species can be identified, allowing a more precise designation of EFH.

--- SENTENCE 1725 ---
Habitats of intermediate and low value may also be essential, depending on the health of the fish population and the ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 1726 ---
For example, if a species is overfished, and habitat loss or degradation is thought to contribute to its overfished condition, all habitats currently used by the species may be essential.

--- SENTENCE 1727 ---
The EFH provisions are especially important because of the procedural requirements they impose on both Councils and federal agencies.

--- SENTENCE 1728 ---
First, for each FMP, Councils must identify adverse impacts to EFH resulting from both fishing and non-fishing activities, and describe measures to minimize these impacts.

--- SENTENCE 1729 ---
Second, the provisions allowed Councils to provide comments and make recommendations to federal or state agencies that propose actions that may affect the habitat, including EFH, of a managed species.

--- SENTENCE 1730 ---
In 2002, NMFS revised the guidelines by providing additional clarifications and guidance to ease implementation of the EFH provision by Councils.

#### **6.2 EFH Designations**

The following EFH designations were developed by the Council and approved by the Secretary of Commerce.

--- SENTENCE 1731 ---
EFH designations for Bottomfish and Seamount Groundfish, Crustaceans, Precious Corals and Pelagic MUS were approved by the Secretary on February 3, 1999 (64 FR 19068).

--- SENTENCE 1732 ---
EFH designations for Coral Reef Ecosystem MUS were approved by the Secretary on June 14, 2002 (69 FR 8336).

--- SENTENCE 1733 ---
For the purpose of this plan, Pelagics MUS are not part of the American Samoa Archipelago FEP MUS.

--- SENTENCE 1734 ---
In describing and identifying EFH for Bottomfish and Seamount Groundfish, Crustacean, Precious Coral, Coral Reef Ecosystem, and Pelagic MUS, four alternatives were considered: (1) designate EFH based on the best available scientific information (preferred alternative), (2) designate all waters EFH, (3) designate a minimal area as EFH, and (4) no action.

--- SENTENCE 1735 ---
Ultimately, the Council selected Alternative 1 designate EFH based on observed habitat utilization patterns in localized areas as the preferred alternative.

--- SENTENCE 1736 ---
This alternative was preferred by the Council for three reasons.

--- SENTENCE 1737 ---
First, it adhered to the intent of the MSA provisions and to the guidelines that have been set out through regulations and expanded on by NMFS because the best available scientific data were used to make carefully considered designations.

--- SENTENCE 1738 ---
Second, it resulted in more precise designations of EFH at the species complex level than would be the case if Alternative 2 were chosen.

--- SENTENCE 1739 ---
At the same time, it did not run the risk of being arbitrary and capricious as would be the case if Alternative 3 were chosen.

--- SENTENCE 1740 ---
Finally, it recognized that EFH designation is an ongoing process and set out a procedure for reviewing and refining EFH designations as more information on species' habitat requirements becomes available.

--- SENTENCE 1741 ---
The Council has used the best available scientific information to describe EFH in text and tables that provide information on the biological requirements for each life stage (egg, larvae, juvenile, adult) of all MUS (see the Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council's Essential

Fish Habitat Descriptions for Western Pacific Archipelagic and Remote Island Areas Fishery Ecosystem Plan Management Unit Species).

--- SENTENCE 1742 ---
Careful judgment was used in determining the extent of the essential fish habitat that should be designated to ensure that sufficient habitat in good condition is available to maintain a sustainable fishery and the managed species' contribution to a healthy ecosystem.

--- SENTENCE 1743 ---
Because there are large gaps in scientific knowledge about the life histories and habitat requirements of many MUS in the Western Pacific Region, the Council adopted a precautionary approach in designating EFH to ensure that enough habitats are protected to sustain managed species.

--- SENTENCE 1744 ---
The preferred depth ranges of specific life stages were used to designate EFH for bottomfish and crustaceans.

--- SENTENCE 1745 ---
In the case of crustaceans, the designation was further refined based on productivity data.

--- SENTENCE 1746 ---
The precious corals designation combines depth and bottom type as indicators, but it is further refined based on the known distribution of the most productive areas for these organisms.

--- SENTENCE 1747 ---
Species were grouped into complexes because available information suggests that many of them occur together and share similar habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1748 ---
In addition to the narratives, the general distribution and geographic limits of EFH for each life history stage are presented in the forms of maps..

--- SENTENCE 1749 ---
The Council incorporated these data into a geographic information system to facilitate analysis and presentation.

--- SENTENCE 1750 ---
More detailed and informative maps will be produced as more complete information about population responses to habitat characteristics (e.g. growth, survival or reproductive rates) becomes available.

--- SENTENCE 1751 ---
At the time the Council's EFH designations were approved by the Secretary, there was not enough data on the relative productivity of different habitats to develop EFH designations based on Level 3 or Level 4 data for any of the Western Pacific Council's MUS.

--- SENTENCE 1752 ---
Council adopted a fifth level, denoted Level 0, for situations in which there is no information available about the geographic extent of a particular managed species' life stage.

--- SENTENCE 1753 ---
Subsequently, very limited habitat information has been made available for MUS for the Council to review and use to revise the initial EFH designations previously approved by the Secretary.

--- SENTENCE 1754 ---
However, habitat-related studies for bottomfish and precious coral and to a limited extent, crustaceans, are currently ongoing in the NWHI and MHI.

--- SENTENCE 1755 ---
Additionally, fish and benthic surveys conducted during the NMFS Coral Reef Ecosystem Division's Pacific-Wide Rapid Assessment and Monitoring Program, along with other near-shore coral reef habitat health assessments undertaken by other agencies, may provide additional information to refine EFH designations for Coral Reef Ecosystem MUS in all island areas, including the American Samoa archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 1756 ---
#### **6.2.1 Bottomfish**

Except for several of the major commercial species, very little is known about the life histories, habitat utilization patterns, food habits, or spawning behavior of most adult bottomfish and seamount groundfish species.

--- SENTENCE 1757 ---
Furthermore, very little is known about the distribution and habitat requirements of juvenile bottomfish.

--- SENTENCE 1758 ---
Generally, the distribution of adult bottomfish in the western Pacific region is closely linked to suitable physical habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1759 ---
Unlike the U.S. mainland with its continental shelf ecosystems, Pacific islands are primarily volcanic peaks with steep drop-offs and limited shelf ecosystems.

--- SENTENCE 1760 ---
The

BMUS under the Council's jurisdiction are found concentrated on the steep slopes of deepwater banks.

--- SENTENCE 1761 ---
The 100-fathom isobath is commonly used as an index of bottomfish habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1762 ---
Adult bottomfish are usually found in habitats characterized by a hard substrate of high structural complexity.

--- SENTENCE 1763 ---
The total extent and geographic distribution of the preferred habitat of bottomfish is not well known.

--- SENTENCE 1764 ---
Bottomfish populations are not evenly distributed within their natural habitat; instead, they are found dispersed in a non-random, patchy fashion.

--- SENTENCE 1765 ---
Deepwater snappers tend to aggregate in association with prominent underwater features, such as headlands and promontories.

--- SENTENCE 1766 ---
There is regional variation in species composition, as well as a relative abundance of the MUS of the deepwater bottomfish complex in the Western Pacific Region.

--- SENTENCE 1767 ---
In American Samoa, Guam, and the Northern Mariana Islands, the bottomfish fishery can be divided into two distinct fisheries: a shallow- and a deep-water bottomfish fishery, based on species and depth.

--- SENTENCE 1768 ---
The shallow-water (0–100 m) bottomfish complex comprises groupers, snappers, and jacks in the genera *Lethrinus*, *Lutjanus*, *Epinephelus*, *Aprion*, *Caranx, Variola,* and *Cephalopholis.*

--- SENTENCE 1769 ---
The deep-water (100–400 m) bottomfish complex comprises primarily snappers and groupers in the genera *Pristipomoides, Etelis, Aphareus, Epinephelus,* and *Cephalopholis*.

--- SENTENCE 1770 ---
In Hawaii, the bottomfish fishery targets several species of eteline snappers, carangids, and a single species of groupers.

--- SENTENCE 1771 ---
The target species are generally found at depths of 50–270 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1772 ---
To reduce the complexity and the number of EFH identifications required for individual species and life stages, the Council has designated EFH for bottomfish assemblages pursuant to Section 600.805(b) of 62 FR 66551.

--- SENTENCE 1773 ---
The species complex designations include deep-slope bottomfish (shallow water and deep water) and seamount groundfish complexes.

--- SENTENCE 1774 ---
The designation of these complexes is based on the ecological relationships among species and their preferred habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1775 ---
These species complexes are grouped by the known depth distributions of individual BMUS throughout the Western Pacific Region.

--- SENTENCE 1776 ---
These are summarized in Table 16.

--- SENTENCE 1777 ---
For a broader description of the life history and habitat utilization patterns of individual BMUS, see the Western Pacific Fishery Management Council's Essential Fish Habitat Descriptions for Western Pacific Archipelagic and Remote Island Areas Fishery Ecosystem Plan Management Unit Species.

--- SENTENCE 1778 ---
At present, there is not enough data on the relative productivity of different habitats to develop EFH designations based on Level 3 or Level 4 data.

--- SENTENCE 1779 ---
Given the uncertainty concerning the life histories and habitat requirements of many BMUS, the Council designated EFH for adult and juvenile bottomfish as the water column and all bottom habitat extending from the shoreline to a depth of 400 meters (200 fathoms) encompassing the steep drop-offs and high-relief habitats that are important for bottomfish throughout the Western Pacific Region.

--- SENTENCE 1780 ---
The eggs and larvae of all BMUS are pelagic, floating at the surface until hatching and subject thereafter to advection by the prevailing ocean currents.

--- SENTENCE 1781 ---
There have been few taxonomic studies of these life stages of snappers (lutjanids) and groupers (epinepheline serranids).

--- SENTENCE 1782 ---
Presently, few larvae can be identified to species.

--- SENTENCE 1783 ---
As snapper and grouper larvae are rarely collected in plankton surveys, it is extremely difficult to study their distribution.

--- SENTENCE 1784 ---
Because of the existing scientific uncertainty about the distribution of the eggs and larvae of bottomfish, the Council designated

the water column extending from the shoreline to the outer boundary of the EEZ to a depth of 400 meters as EFH for bottomfish eggs and larvae throughout the Western Pacific Region.

--- SENTENCE 1785 ---
In the past, a large-scale foreign seamount groundfish fishery extended throughout the southeastern reaches of the northern Hawaiian Ridge.

--- SENTENCE 1786 ---
The seamount groundfish complex consists of three species (pelagic armorheads, alfonsins, and ratfish).

--- SENTENCE 1787 ---
These species dwell at 200–600 meters on the submarine slopes and summits of seamounts.

--- SENTENCE 1788 ---
A collapse of the seamount groundfish stocks has resulted in a greatly reduced yield in recent years.

--- SENTENCE 1789 ---
Although a moratorium on the harvest of the seamount groundfish within the EEZ has been in place since 1986, no substantial recovery of the stocks has been observed.

--- SENTENCE 1790 ---
Historically, there has been no domestic seamount groundfish fishery.

--- SENTENCE 1791 ---
The life histories and distributional patterns of seamount groundfish are also poorly understood.

--- SENTENCE 1792 ---
Data are lacking on the effects of oceanographic variability on migration and recruitment of individual management unit species.

--- SENTENCE 1793 ---
On the basis of the best available data, the Council designated the EFH for the adult life stage of the seamount groundfish complex as all waters and bottom habitat bounded by latitude 29°–35° N and longitude 171° E–179° W between 80–600 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1794 ---
EFH for eggs, larvae, and juveniles is the epipelagic zone (~200 m) of all waters bounded by latitude 29°–35° N and longitude 171° E–179°

--- SENTENCE 1795 ---
W. This EFH designation encompasses the Hancock Seamounts, part of the northern extent of the Hawaiian Ridge, located 1,500 nautical miles northwest of Honolulu.

#### **6.2.2 Crustaceans**

Spiny lobsters are found throughout the Indo-Pacific region.

--- SENTENCE 1796 ---
All spiny lobsters in the western Pacific region belong to the family Palinuridae.

--- SENTENCE 1797 ---
The slipper lobsters belong to the closely related family, Scyllaridae.

--- SENTENCE 1798 ---
There are 13 species of the genus *Panulirus* distributed in the tropical and subtropical Pacific between 35° N and 35° S. *P. penicillatus* is the most widely distributed, the other three species are absent from the waters of many island nations of the region.

--- SENTENCE 1799 ---
The Hawaiian spiny lobster (*P. marginatus*) is endemic to Hawaii and the Johnston Atoll and is the primary species of interest in the NWHI fishery, the principal commercial lobster fishery in the western Pacific region.

--- SENTENCE 1800 ---
This fishery also targets the slipper lobster *Scyllarides squammosus.

--- SENTENCE 1801 ---
Three other species of lobster—pronghorn spiny lobster (*Panulirus pencillatus*), ridgeback slipper lobster (*Scyllarides haanii*), and Chinese slipper lobster (*Parribacus antarticus*)—and the Kona crab, family Raninidae, are taken in low numbers in the NWHI fishery.

--- SENTENCE 1802 ---
In the NWHI, there is wide variation in lobster total density, size, and sex ratio among the different islands.

--- SENTENCE 1803 ---
Neither the extent of species interaction between *P. marginatus* and *Scyllarides squammosus* nor the role of density dependent factors in controlling population abundance is known.

--- SENTENCE 1804 ---
In the Main Hawaiian Islands (MHI), most of the commercial, recreational, and subsistence catches of spiny lobster are taken from waters under state jurisdiction.

--- SENTENCE 1805 ---
*P. maginatus* and *P. pencillatus* are taken in nearly equal numbers in trap samples around the island of Oahu.

--- SENTENCE 1806 ---
However, the species composition or the magnitude of the subsistence, recreational, and commercial catch is not known.

--- SENTENCE 1807 ---
In America Samoa, the Northern Mariana Islands, and Guam, the species composition or the magnitude of the subsistence, recreational, and commercial catch is also unknown.

--- SENTENCE 1808 ---
In Hawaii, adult spiny lobsters are typically found on rocky substrate in well-protected areas, in crevices, and under rocks.

--- SENTENCE 1809 ---
Unlike many other species of *Panulirus*, the juveniles and adults of *P. marginatus* are not found in separate habitats apart from one another.

--- SENTENCE 1810 ---
Juvenile *P. marginatus* recruit directly to adult habitat; they do not utilize a separate shallow-water nursery habitat apart from the adults as do many Palinurid lobsters.

--- SENTENCE 1811 ---
Similarly, juvenile and adult *P. pencillatus* also share the same habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1812 ---
*P. marginatus* is found seaward of the reefs and within the lagoons and atolls of the islands.

--- SENTENCE 1813 ---
The reported depth distribution of *P. marginatus* is 3–200 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1814 ---
While this species is found down to depths of 200 meters, it usually inhabits shallower waters.

--- SENTENCE 1815 ---
*P. marginatus* is most abundant in waters of 90 meters or less.

--- SENTENCE 1816 ---
Large adult spiny lobsters are captured at depths as shallow as 3 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1817 ---
In the southwestern Pacific, spiny lobsters are typically found in association with coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 1818 ---
Coral reefs provide shelter as well as a diverse and abundant supply of food items.

--- SENTENCE 1819 ---
*Panulirus pencillatus* inhabits the rocky shelters in the windward surf zones of oceanic reefs and moves on to the reef flat at night to forage.

--- SENTENCE 1820 ---
Very little is known about the planktonic phase of the phyllosoma larvae of *Panulirus marginatus*.

--- SENTENCE 1821 ---
The oceanographic and physiographic features that result in the retention of lobster larvae within the Hawaiian archipelago are poorly understood.

--- SENTENCE 1822 ---
Evidence suggests that fine-scale oceanographic features, such as eddies and currents, serve to retain phyllosoma larvae within the Hawaiian Island chain.

--- SENTENCE 1823 ---
While there is a wide range of lobster densities between banks within the NWHI, the spatial distribution of phyllosoma larvae appears to be homogenous (Polovina and Moffitt 1995).

--- SENTENCE 1824 ---
To reduce the complexity and the number of EFH identifications required for individual species and life stages, the Council has designated EFH for crustacean species assemblages.

--- SENTENCE 1825 ---
The species complex designations are spiny and slipper lobsters and Kona crab.

--- SENTENCE 1826 ---
The designation of these complexes is based on the ecological relationships among species and their preferred habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1827 ---
For a broader description of the life history and habitat utilization patterns of individual CMUS, see the Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council's Essential Fish Habitat Descriptions for Western Pacific Archipelagic and Remote Island Areas Fishery Ecosystem Plan Management Unit Species.

--- SENTENCE 1828 ---
At present, there is not enough data on the relative productivity of different habitats of CMUS to develop EFH designations based on Level 3 or Level 4 data.

--- SENTENCE 1829 ---
There are little data concerning growth rates, reproductive potentials, and natural mortality rates at the various life history stages.

--- SENTENCE 1830 ---
The relationship between egg production, larval settlement, and stock recruitment is also poorly understood.

--- SENTENCE 1831 ---
Although there is a paucity of data on the preferred depth distribution of phyllosoma larvae in Hawaii, the depth distribution of phyllosoma larvae of other species of *Panulirus* common in the Indo-Pacific region has been documented.

--- SENTENCE 1832 ---
Later stages of panulirid phyllosoma larvae have been found at depths between 80 and 120 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1833 ---
For these reasons, the Council

designated EFH for spiny lobster larvae as the water column from the shoreline to the outer limit of the EEZ down to a depth of 150 meters throughout the Western Pacific Region.

--- SENTENCE 1834 ---
The EFH for juvenile and adult spiny lobster is designated as the bottom habitat from the shoreline to a depth of 100 meters throughout the Western Pacific Region (see Table 16).

#### **6.2.3 Precious Corals**

In the Hawaiian Islands, precious coral beds have been found only in the deep interisland channels and off promontories at depths between 300 and 1,500 meters and 30 and 100 meters.

--- SENTENCE 1835 ---
The six known beds of pink, gold, and bamboo corals are Keahole Point, Makapuu, Kaena Point, Wespac, Brooks Bank, and 180 Fathom Bank.

--- SENTENCE 1836 ---
Makapuu is the only bed that has been surveyed accurately enough to estimate MSY.

--- SENTENCE 1837 ---
The Wespac bed, located between Necker and Nihoa Islands in the NWHI, has been set aside for use in baseline studies and as a possible reproductive reserve.

--- SENTENCE 1838 ---
The harvesting of precious corals is prohibited in this area.

--- SENTENCE 1839 ---
Within the western Pacific region, the only directed fishery for precious corals has occurred in the Hawaiian Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1840 ---
At present, there is no commercial harvesting of precious corals in the EEZ, but several firms have expressed interest.

--- SENTENCE 1841 ---
Precious corals may be divided into deep- and shallow-water species.

--- SENTENCE 1842 ---
Deep-water precious corals are generally found between 350 and 1,500 meters and include pink coral (*Corallium secundum*), gold coral (*Gerardia* sp. and *Parazoanthus* sp.), and bamboo coral (*Lepidistis olapa*).

--- SENTENCE 1843 ---
Shallowwater species occur between 30 and 100 meters and consist primarily of three species of black coral: *Antipathes dichotoma*, *Antipathes grandis,* and *Antipathes ulex*.

--- SENTENCE 1844 ---
In Hawaii, *Antipathes dichotoma* accounts for around 90 percent of the commercial harvest of black coral, and virtually all of it is harvested in state waters.

--- SENTENCE 1845 ---
Precious corals are non–reef building and inhabit depth zones below the euphotic zone.

--- SENTENCE 1846 ---
They are found on solid substrate in areas that are swept relatively clean by moderate-to-strong (> 25 cm/sec) bottom currents.

--- SENTENCE 1847 ---
Strong currents help prevent the accumulation of sediments, which would smother young coral colonies and prevent settlement of new larvae.

--- SENTENCE 1848 ---
Precious coral yields tend to be higher in areas of shell sandstone, limestone, and basaltic or metamorphic rock with a limestone veneer.

--- SENTENCE 1849 ---
Black corals are most frequently found under vertical drop-offs.

--- SENTENCE 1850 ---
Such features are common off Kauai and Maui in the MHI, suggesting that their abundance is related to suitable habitat (Grigg 1976).

--- SENTENCE 1851 ---
Off Oahu, many submarine terraces that otherwise would be suitable habitat for black corals are covered with sediments.

--- SENTENCE 1852 ---
In the MHI, the lower depth range of *Antipathes dichotoma* and *A. grandis* coincides with the top of the thermocline (ca. 100 m; Grigg 1983).

--- SENTENCE 1853 ---
Pink, bamboo, and gold corals all have planktonic larval stages and sessile adult stages.

--- SENTENCE 1854 ---
Larvae settle on solid substrate where they form colonial branching colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1855 ---
The length of the larval stage of all species of precious corals is unknown.

--- SENTENCE 1856 ---
The habitat sustaining precious corals is generally in pristine condition.

--- SENTENCE 1857 ---
There are no known areas that have sustained damage due to resource exploitation, notwithstanding the alleged illegal heavy foreign fishing for corals in the Hancock Seamounts area.

--- SENTENCE 1858 ---
To reduce the complexity and the number of EFH identifications required for individual species and life stages, the Council designated EFH for precious coral assemblages.

--- SENTENCE 1859 ---
The species complex designations are deep- and shallow-water complexes (see Table 16).

--- SENTENCE 1860 ---
The designation of these complexes is based on the ecological relationships among the individual species and their preferred habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1861 ---
The Council considered using the known depth range of individual PCMUS to designate EFH, but rejected this alternative because of the rarity of the occurrence of suitable habitat conditions.

--- SENTENCE 1862 ---
Instead, the Council designated the six known beds of precious corals as EFH.

--- SENTENCE 1863 ---
The Council believes that the narrow EFH designation will facilitate the consultation process.

--- SENTENCE 1864 ---
In addition, the Council designated three black coral beds in the MHI—between Milolii and South Point on Hawaii, Auau Channel between Maui and Lanai, and the southern border of Kauai—as EFH.

#### **6.2.4 Coral Reef Ecosystems**

In designating EFH for Coral Reef Ecosystem MUS, the Council used an approach similar to one used by both the South Atlantic and the Pacific Fishery Management Councils.

--- SENTENCE 1865 ---
Using this approach, MUS are linked to specific habitat "composites" (e.g. sand, live coral, seagrass beds, mangrove, open ocean) for each life history stage, consistent with the depth of the ecosystem to 50 fathoms and to the limit of the EEZ.

--- SENTENCE 1866 ---
These designations could also protect species managed under other Council FMPs to the degree that they share these habitats.

--- SENTENCE 1867 ---
Except for several of the major coral reef associated species, very little is known about the life histories, habitat utilization patterns, food habits, or spawning behavior of most coral reef associated species.

--- SENTENCE 1868 ---
For this reason, the Council, through the CRE FMP, designated EFH using a two-tiered approach based on the division of MUS into the Currently Harvested Coral Reef Taxa (CHCRT) and Potentially Harvested Coral Reef Taxa (PHCRT) categories.

--- SENTENCE 1869 ---
This is also consistent with the use of habitat composites.

--- SENTENCE 1870 ---
#### **Currently Harvested Coral Reef Taxa MUS**

In the first tier, EFH has been identified for species that (a) are currently being harvested in state and federal waters and for which some fishery information is available and (b) are likely to be targeted in the near future based on historical catch data.

--- SENTENCE 1871 ---
Tables 11 and 12 summarize the habitat types used by CHCRT species.

--- SENTENCE 1872 ---
To reduce the complexity and the number of EFH identifications required for individual species and life stages, the Council has designated EFH for species assemblages pursuant to 50 CFR 600.815 (a)(2)(ii)(E).

--- SENTENCE 1873 ---
The designation of these complexes is based on the ecological relationships among species and their preferred habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1874 ---
These species complexes are grouped by the known depth distributions of individual MUS.

--- SENTENCE 1875 ---
The EFH designations for CHCRT throughout the Western Pacific Region are summarized in Table 13.

--- SENTENCE 1876 ---
For a broader description of the life history and habitat utilization patterns of CHCRT, see the Western Pacific Regional Fishery Management Council's Essential Fish Habitat Descriptions for Western Pacific Archipelagic and Remote Island Areas Fishery Ecosystem Plan Management Unit Species.

#### **Potentially Harvested Coral Reef Taxa MUS**

EFH has also been designated for the second tier, PHCRT.

--- SENTENCE 1877 ---
These taxa include literally thousands of species encompassing almost all coral reef fauna and flora.

--- SENTENCE 1878 ---
However, there is very little scientific knowledge about the life histories and habitat requirements of the thousands of species of organisms that compose these taxa.

--- SENTENCE 1879 ---
In fact, a large percentage of these biota have not been described by science.

--- SENTENCE 1880 ---
Therefore, the Council has used the precautionary approach in designating EFH for PHCRT so that enough habitat is protected to sustain managed species.

--- SENTENCE 1881 ---
Table 14 summarizes the habitat types used by PHCRT species.

--- SENTENCE 1882 ---
The designation of EFH for PHCRT throughout the Western Pacific Region is summarized in Table 15.

--- SENTENCE 1883 ---
As with CHCRT, the Council has designated EFH for species assemblages pursuant to the federal regulations cited above.

--- SENTENCE 1884 ---
See the Western Pacific Fishery Management Council's Essential Fish Habitat Descriptions for Western Pacific Archipelagic and Remote Island Areas Fishery Ecosystem Plan Management Unit Species for more detailed descriptions of life history and habitat utilization patterns of PHCRT.

--- SENTENCE 1885 ---
Occurrence of Currently Harvested Management Unit Species** 

**Habitats:** Mangrove (Ma), Lagoon (La), Estuarine (Es), Seagrass Beds (SB), Soft substrate (Ss), Coral Reef/Hard Substrate (Cr/Hr), Patch Reefs (Pr), Surge Zone (Sz), Deep-Slope Terraces (DST), Pelagic/Open Ocean (Pe)

**Life history stages:**

--- SENTENCE 1886 ---

--- SENTENCE 2071 ---
| All Potentially Harvested Coral Reef Taxa | EFH for all life stages of Potentially Harvest water column and bottom habitat from the shadepth of 50 fm.

--- SENTENCE 2072 ---
| C                        |

#### **6.3 HAPC Designations**

In addition to EFH, the Council identified habitat areas of particular concern (HAPCs) within EFH for all FMPs.

--- SENTENCE 2073 ---
HAPCs are specific areas within EFH that are essential to the life cycle of important coral reef species.

--- SENTENCE 2074 ---
In determining whether a type or area of EFH should be designated as an HAPC, one or more of the following criteria established by NMFS must be met: (a) the ecological function provided by the habitat is important; (b) the habitat is sensitive to humaninduced environmental degradation; (c) development activities are, or will be, stressing the habitat type; or (c) the habitat type is rare.

--- SENTENCE 2075 ---
However, it is important to note that if an area meets only one of the HAPC criteria, it will not necessarily be designated an HAPC.

--- SENTENCE 2076 ---
Table 16 summarizes the EFH and HAPC designations for all Western Pacific Archipelagic FEP MUS, including American Samoa Archipelago FEP MUS.

#### **6.3.1 Bottomfish**

On the basis of the known distribution and habitat requirements of adult bottomfish, the Council designated all escarpments/slopes between 40–280 meters throughout the Western Pacific Region, including American Samoa, as bottomfish HAPC.

--- SENTENCE 2077 ---
In addition, the Council designated the three known areas of juvenile opakapaka habitat (two off Oahu and one off Molokai) as HAPC.

--- SENTENCE 2078 ---
The basis for this designation is the ecological function that these areas provide, the rarity of the habitat, and the susceptibility of these areas to human-induced environmental degradation.

--- SENTENCE 2079 ---
Off Oahu, juvenile snappers occupy a flat, open bottom of primarily soft substrate in depths ranging from 40 to 73 meters.

--- SENTENCE 2080 ---
This habitat is quite different from that utilized by adult snappers.

--- SENTENCE 2081 ---
Surveys suggest that the preferred habitat of juvenile opakapaka in the waters around Hawaii represents only a small fraction of the total habitat at the appropriate depths.

--- SENTENCE 2082 ---
Areas of flat featureless bottom have typically been thought of as providing low-value fishery habitat.

--- SENTENCE 2083 ---
It is possible that juvenile snappers occur in other habitat types, but in such low densities that they have yet to be observed.

--- SENTENCE 2084 ---
The recent discovery of concentrations of juvenile snappers in relatively shallow water and featureless bottom habitat indicates the need for more research to help identify, map, and study nursery habitat for juvenile snapper.

#### **6.3.2

--- SENTENCE 2085 ---
Crustaceans**

Currently, no crustacean HAPC has been designated in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2086 ---
Research indicates that banks with summits less than 30 meters support successful recruitment of juvenile spiny lobster while those with summit deeper than 30 meters do not.

--- SENTENCE 2087 ---
For this reason, the Council has designated all banks in the NWHI with summits less than 30 meters as HAPC.

--- SENTENCE 2088 ---
The basis for designating these areas as HAPC is the ecological function provided, the rarity of the habitat type, and the susceptibility of these areas to human-induced environmental degradation.

--- SENTENCE 2089 ---
The complex relationship between recruitment sources and sinks of spiny lobsters is poorly

understood.

--- SENTENCE 2090 ---
The Council feels that in the absence of a better understanding of these relationships, the adoption of a precautionary approach to protect and conserve habitat is warranted.

--- SENTENCE 2091 ---
The relatively long pelagic larval phase for palinurids results in very wide dispersal of spiny lobster larvae.

--- SENTENCE 2092 ---
Palinurid larvae are transported up to 2,000 nautical miles by prevailing ocean currents.

--- SENTENCE 2093 ---
Because phyllosoma larvae are transported by the prevailing ocean currents outside of EEZ waters, the Council has identified habitat in these areas as "important habitat.

--- SENTENCE 2094 ---
"

#### **6.3.3 Precious Corals**

Currently, no precious coral HAPC has been designated in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2095 ---
#### **6.3.4 Coral Reef Ecosystems**

Because of the already-noted lack of scientific data, the Council considered locations that are known to support populations of Coral Reef Ecosystem MUS and meet NMFS criteria for HAPC.

--- SENTENCE 2096 ---
Although not one of the criteria established by NMFS, the Council considered designating areas that are already protected—for example, wildlife refuges—as HAPC.

--- SENTENCE 2097 ---
The Coral Reef Ecosystem MUS HAPCs for American Samoa identified in Table 17have met at least one of the four criteria listed above, or the fifth criterion just identified.

--- SENTENCE 2098 ---
However, a great deal of life history work needs to be done in order to adequately identify the extent of HAPCs and link them to particular species or life stages.
